- A border on one side of a rounded element follows the corners at its ends and thins out where it meets a side without a border, as CSS draws it. It was a square band that stuck out past the rounded corners.
- A border outside or centered on a rounded element has the element's corners on its inner edge and is rounder on its outer edge by its width. Its outer edge had the element's radius and its inner edge a smaller one, which left a gap between the border and the element's background at each corner.
- Where the sides of a border have different widths, each inner corner is shaped by the two sides that meet there. Every inner corner was cut by the widest side of the four.
- A side of a border that has no width paints nothing at its edge. At a fraction of a pixel, as on a display scaled by 1.25 or 1.5, the three other sides of a border could leave a faint line there.
- `Border` and `ResolvedBorder` have a new public field, `dash`, and `RectCmd` a new public field, `stroke_style`: code that builds one with every field named must add it. `Border::new`, `inside`, `outside` and `center` are unchanged. A custom `RenderEngine` that ignores `RectCmd::stroke_style` draws dashed borders solid and the corners of an outside border as before.
- `PaletteColor` has a new variant, `Foreground`, and `ThemePalette` a new public field, `foreground`: code that matches the enum without a wildcard arm, or builds a `ThemePalette` with every field named, must add them (`..ThemePalette::default()` takes care of the field).
- An open layer is the layer the same declaration opened in the last pass, wherever it is among the others. Layers without a key were matched by their place, so when the first of two open popups closed, the second took over the first one's scroll offset, focus and node ids. Layers are still matched by place when the declaring element is new. A layer that plays a keyframe animation or has an exit animation is never matched to another declaration's layer: a popup opened as another closes is mounted anew.
- On a machine whose user asked the system for less motion, transitions of place and size no longer animate, sheets and stack navigation included. `Theme::set_reduced_motion(Some(false))` restores them.
- A transition of a color given as a `PaletteColor` role now runs. The value a node is heading to was read from concrete colors only, so a background or border set to a role changed at once whatever its transition said. A role whose color changes with the theme runs the transition as well.
- `AnimatableProperty` has three new variants and `AnimatableValue` two: code that matches either without a wildcard arm must add them.
- `TextStyle` and `MarkdownTextStyle` have a new public field, `strikethrough`: code that builds one with every field named must add it (`..TextStyle::default()` takes care of it).
- Markdown draws `~~struck~~` text with a line through it: `ThemeMarkdown::strikethrough` now sets a strikethrough by default. Set it to `MarkdownInlineStyle::default()` for the old look.
- A plain `Text` whose style has an `underline` now draws it.
- `LayerCmd` has a new public field, `blur: Option<LayerBlur>`, `RenderList` a new public field, `backdrops: Vec<BackdropCmd>`, `AnimatableProperty` two new variants and `QuadContent` a new variant, `Backdrop`: code that builds a `LayerCmd` or a `RenderList` with every field named, or matches `AnimatableProperty` or `QuadContent` without a wildcard arm, must add them. A custom `RenderEngine` that ignores `RenderList::backdrops` draws the frame without its backdrop blurs. A custom `RenderEngine` that paints `RenderList::layers` should paint a blurred layer for `LayerCmd::painted()` (larger than `bounds`, and possibly outside the window) and blur it by `LayerBlur::sigma`; one that ignores the field draws the layer unblurred, clipped to `bounds`.
- A layer around a blurred one covers the blur's spread, so `LayerCmd::bounds` of a faded group can be larger than its own rects and glyphs.
- A glyph whose ink reaches into the clip from a line of text that lies outside it is now painted whenever its text is, for plain texts under 1,024 bytes. Before, it was painted only if the text had first been painted unclipped, and dropped if the text was first painted while the clip cut it.
- Plain texts under 1,024 bytes stay shaped in a cache of their own, up to 4 MiB a glyph engine, beside the 48 MiB cache that now holds documents only. `GlyphEngineProfile::plain_cache_bytes` and `plain_cache_budget` cover both, so the budget reads 4 MiB higher. The text caches of the chat screen hold 3.2 MB for its settled frame where they held 1.8 MB.
- `RenderProfile::draw_calls` is lower for a frame of several texts; the pixels are the same.
- `SelectTriggerState` has two new public fields, `leading` and `trailing`: code that builds one with every field named must add them.
- A backdrop blur is not run again while what is behind its element stays the same. The wgpu and the DX12 renderer keep the blurred pixels and put them back, with two draws inside the target's pass, in every frame in which the blur reads the same pixels: the same rectangle around the element's box at the same radius and scale, in the same window or layer, with the same things painted before the element where they can reach that rectangle. A frame that changes nothing behind any glass (a hover elsewhere, a caret, content over the glass, the glass's own opacity or corners) takes no copy, no blur pass and no pooled texture for it. A change behind a glass blurs that glass again, and whatever glass lies over it. Glass that can reach a video frame in a native texture, a `Canvas` or an external wgpu texture is blurred every frame, as before, since those change without the frame's draws changing. A window keeps at most 64 blurs, in textures of at most twice the bytes of its own pixels (1 MiB for a small window), and gives a texture back to the pool when its glass is gone, has moved or changed size, or the window is resized. The rule is spelled out in [Blur](docs/src/content/docs/blur.md#when-a-backdrop-is-blurred-again).
- A change of the hover asks for a frame only when it shows. The hover moves whenever the pointer comes onto another node (from a row onto the text inside it, for one), and every such move set `Tree::needs_redraw()`, so the pointer crossing a list of rows that look the same under it drew a frame for each move: 78 frames a second in `examples/hover_frames_probe.rs`, none of them different from the one before. A frame is now asked for when a node that gained or lost the hover has a `hovered` style (or any state style that changes frame, padding or flex, which lays it out again), is a checkbox, a slider, a select or a Markdown link, scrolls (its scrollbar may show for the pointer, and loses its hovered style with it), or ran an `on_mouse_enter` or `on_mouse_leave` handler; and for every change of the hover while DevTools is open on the window, which shows it. In the example the plain list draws 1 frame in 10 s (the pointer entering the list) and a list whose rows have a `hovered` style 38 a second, one per row the pointer comes onto, down from 78. The hover, its handlers, `Tree::cursor()`, `ElementRef::hovered()` and `InteractionState::is_hovered()` are up to date after every pointer move, as before. What relied on the frame a hover used to draw no longer gets one where the hover shows nothing: a shell that drives a `Tree` itself reads `Tree::cursor()` after each pointer event (the winit shell already does) rather than after a pass only, and a component that renders again on hover sets a signal from its hover handlers, since `ElementRef::hovered()` and `InteractionState::is_hovered()` are plain reads that render nothing.

Fixes:

- A variable font is drawn at the weight asked for. The platform's own font on a Mac and a phone is one variable face named Regular, and every weight was matched to that name, so nothing was bold.
- A scroll container's own padding was taken from its content's width and then not applied to where the content starts.
- A title bar given a height of its own is that high for the content under it.
- A wide blur against the window's left or right edge is no longer flat along it. A blur that runs on a reduced copy read the copy's outermost texel for the last half of it, so the pixels next to the window's edge repeated their neighbour: with a sharp edge 6 pixels from the window's, the first pixel under `backdrop_blur(24.0)` was 3 levels of 255 off. The reduced copy now has a texel of the repeated edge around it, and all four edges are read the same way.

Dependencies:

- cosmic-text is built with its `shape-run-cache` feature. Its cache of shaped words is trimmed by the glyph engine (every 256 passes or newly laid out texts, and emptied when fonts change) and is not part of `GlyphEngineProfile`'s byte counts.

Internal:

- `FrameSource` is the one place the shell asks for and takes frames, on every platform.
- The workspace builds and its tests pass on macOS and in an iOS simulator.
- The `lurq` package names what it publishes (`include` in its manifest), so a file put in the crate's directory stays out of a release unless it is in `src`, `assets`, `tests`, `benches` or `examples`.
- A blur is finished before it is composited. The vertical Gaussian runs on the (reduced) copy into a texture of its own, as the horizontal one does, and the pass that composites a blurred layer or puts a backdrop back reads that texture once per pixel. Before, that pass ran the vertical Gaussian itself at the window's resolution, 25 reads per pixel, or 50 over a reduced copy (two Gaussians, interpolated): `backdrop_blur(32.0)` took about 56 texture reads per pixel of glass and takes about 14. A blur takes one more pass and one more pooled texture for it.
- The wgpu renderer writes the uniforms of a layer composite or a blur pass only when they differ from the ones the layer texture's buffer holds. Every such write is a staging buffer and a copy at the frame's submit, and a faded group, a blurred layer or a glass that holds still has the same passes frame after frame: a frame that blurs four backdrops again took 16 writes for them and takes none.
- `render::glyph_batch` decides which glyph runs share a draw, for both renderers; `glyph_engine::text_cache` and `glyph_engine::shaped_texts` are the two caches of plain text. GPU readback tests draw a scene of clipped, rounded, cut and overlapping texts batched and run by run through wgpu and DX12 and compare the frames pixel for pixel.

## 0.60.0 — 2026-11-11

Added:

- DevTools' **Profiler** tab records real sessions. **Start recording** and **Stop recording** bound a recording that keeps every pass of the inspected windows in between (none skipped, merged or capped to a count), and the tab shows it as a frame timeline on a time axis with a 16.7 ms budget line, a range you select by dragging, and the selected frame's phases and figures. Under it: a **flame chart** (wheel to zoom, drag to pan, click to select), a **call tree** and a **bottom-up** table with total and self times. The tab's buttons are in a toolbar of its own that wraps in a narrow window. See [DevTools](docs/src/content/docs/devtools.md) and [profiling](docs/src/content/docs/profiling.md).
- Recording sessions in the profiling service: `ProfilingHandle::start_recording(RecordingOptions)` keeps every sample until it is ended or reaches `max_bytes` (1 GiB by default), where it stops with `stopped_at_limit` instead of dropping samples. Bounded sessions (`SessionOptions`, MCP's `lurq_profile_*`) work as before.
- Passes carry their phases as spans with start and end times (`PassSample::spans`), so a phase's place in the frame is known, not only its total. `lurq::app::profiler::SpanForest` (`forest_of`, `call_tree`, `bottom_up`) aggregates them outside the UI.
- **Components** (a detailed recording, `RecordingOptions::detailed`): each component render and `after_layout` hook is a span named by the component's type, each pass counts the nodes laid out again per component, and each UI update names what made its components render (a signal, memo, store, context, props or a mount, by type name). Type names only: no values, keys or ids.
- Input latency: an input sample has the delay before its dispatch, the dispatch time, and the time from the shell receiving the event to the present of the first frame after it, with that frame's id. Passes carry their present time, the interval since the previous frame and the vsyncs they missed inside a run of frames, and the reasons the pass ran.
- Counters per captured pass: nodes, draw calls (wgpu and the native DX12 renderer), the runtime's memory estimate and cache sizes, shown as tracks under the timeline.
- `lurq::app::profiler::CountingAllocator`, behind the new `counting_allocator` feature: a global allocator wrapper an application installs in one line, so passes report their allocations, frees and bytes. Without it the allocation track says unavailable.
- GPU time on the wgpu renderer, from timestamp queries issued only while a session is active: the UI and Canvas passes of each frame, matched to its sample by frame id. An adapter without timestamp queries, and the native DX12 renderer, report it unavailable with the reason.
- **Screenshots** in a recording (off by default): at most four presented frames a second, downscaled, with sensitive text covered as in a node screenshot, shown as a filmstrip over the timeline; the frames that paid for a copy are marked.
- Recording files and a Firefox Profiler export: **Save** and **Load** in the tab (`ProfileReport::to_recording_file`, `from_recording_file`), and **Export for Firefox Profiler** (`to_firefox_profile`), which [profiler.firefox.com](https://profiler.firefox.com) opens with its call tree, flame graph and frame, input and UI update markers. The converters are plain functions, without a window or a file dialog.

Behaviour changes:

- The `devtools` feature now enables `perf_profile`. A DevTools build pays the profiler's idle cost on every pass: about 0.2–0.35 µs on the bench's static scene, measured with `cargo bench -p lurq --bench profiler_idle`. A build without `devtools` and `perf_profile` is unchanged.
- The Profiler tab no longer shows the commit list and ranked chart it had; its "what caused this render" list is now the render causes of a detailed recording.
- MCP's profiling JSON stays `schema_version` 1 with fields added to it: `spans`, `dropped_spans`, `reasons`, `present`, `counters`, `screenshot` and `gpu_timing_ms` on passes, `interaction_id`, `presented_frame_id` and `latency_ms` on input samples, and GPU timestamp availability per window. `gpu_timing_ms` and `coverage.gpu_execution` were always null and false and now carry measurements on wgpu. An input sample is published once the frame after it is presented, so its `sequence` follows that pass; its `started_ms` and `completed_ms` are its dispatch, as before.
- `RenderProfile` has a new public field `draw_calls` and `CanvasProfile` a new field `asset_cache_bytes`: code that builds them with every field named must add them.
- The native DX12 renderer reads a frame capture back on a worker thread and waits up to 5 s for the copy, instead of up to 250 ms on the frame's thread.

Internal:

- The current phase of a pass is published without the collector's mutex.
- `scripts/check.ps1` also runs the suite with `devtools,mcp,perf_profile,counting_allocator`.

## 0.59.1 — 2026-11-11

Dependencies:

- wgpu and wgpu-hal are pinned to 29.0.4 (from 29.0.3).
- The `mcp` feature needs rmcp 3.9 or newer (from 3.1). rmcp is re-exported as `lurq::mcp::rmcp`: 3.9 deprecates `rmcp::model::ServerInfo` in favour of `ServerConfig`, the same type under a new name, so an app that names `ServerInfo` through the re-export gets a deprecation warning.

Internal:

- lurq's own lib tests build for feature sets without `raster` (for example `mcp,winit,wgpu`).

## 0.59.0 — 2026-11-11

Added:

- `MarkdownLinkPresentation::pointer_only()`: a Markdown link only the pointer follows. It is no Tab stop and a press on it leaves the focus where it was, so Enter never reaches it and it never shows its focused look, its focus ring or its tooltip from the keyboard; a click (and a middle click, for an app that takes link activations) follows it, and the pointer over it shows the hovered look and the tooltip as on any link. For an app whose reading text is not part of its Tab order. Links are Tab stops as before unless their presentation asks for it.

Fixes:

- The `devtools` feature builds for iOS: `rfd`, which has no iOS backend, is a dependency only on other targets, and the node screenshot's save dialog, its only user, returns no path on iOS.

## 0.58.5 — 2026-11-10

Added:

- `ScrollBarVisibility::OnHover` (`ScrollBarStyle::on_hover()`): the bar of overflowing content is hidden until the pointer is over its scroll container (anywhere in it, not only on the thumb), the content scrolls (the wheel, a scroll request, a text input bringing its caret into view) or its thumb is dragged. Once nothing holds it, it stays for `ScrollBarStyle::fade_delay` (600 ms) and fades out over `fade_duration` (200 ms) along `fade_easing` (`Easing::EASE_OUT`); `ScrollBarStyle::fade(delay, duration)` sets both times, and a zero duration hides it at once. A hidden bar is not painted and takes no pointer, like `Never`; a shown one behaves as an `Auto` bar. It works for scroll containers, `VirtualizedList` and a multiline `TextInput`. The select menu's `menu_scrollbar` was not tried with it.
- `ScrollBarStyle` has three new public fields, `fade_delay`, `fade_duration` and `fade_easing`, and `ScrollBarVisibility` a new variant: code that builds a `ScrollBarStyle` with every field named, or matches `ScrollBarVisibility` without a wildcard arm, must add them.
- `examples/text_input_scrollbar.rs` shows padded multiline inputs with each scrollbar placement and visibility, and can save its own frame (`--capture`).

Behaviour changes:

- A multiline `TextInput` places its scrollbar from its own box, as every scroll container does. Before, the bar was placed from the box inside the input's padding, so a vertical bar sat `padding.right + edge_inset` from the input's right edge and its track ended `padding + end_inset` from the top and bottom edges. Now it sits `edge_inset` from the right edge and its track ends `end_inset` from the top and bottom edges, whatever the padding, and the thumb's drag and the track's press take the pointer there. A `ScrollBarPlacement::Reserved` lane is at the input's right edge, with the right padding between it and the text; the text wraps at the same width as before. An app that moved the bar outward with a negative `edge_inset`, or chose insets to make up for the padding, must set the insets it wants from the input's edge.
- A multiline `TextInput`'s padding scrolls with its text, as a browser's `textarea` does. The text is clipped at the input's top and bottom edges (inside its border) instead of at the padding: scrolled down, lines run through the top padding, and at the start lines run through the bottom padding; the top padding shows over the first line at the start and the bottom padding under the last line at the end. The offsets the text scrolls between are unchanged, and the caret's line is still kept in view inside the padding. A single-line input and scroll containers are unchanged.

Fixes:

- A scroll made through a `ScrollState` is drawn in the next frame, whoever made it (#94). An `on_scroll` handler that called `prevent_default` and scrolled its container itself with `set_scroll` or `set_scroll_pending` showed nothing until something else drew a frame (the pointer moving, a hover change): the built-in scroll, which the handler had prevented, was the only path that asked for one. `Tree::needs_redraw` now also answers for a scroll container that was scrolled, or asked to scroll, since its last layout, so the shell draws the frame after the event; the same holds for a `ScrollState` written from any other handler or between events on the UI thread. A write from another thread still shows only once the window's loop wakes.
- `ScrollState::set_scroll` to the offset the content is already at, and `set_scroll_pending` to that offset with no other scroll queued, no longer mark the layout dirty: a handler that clamps at an edge asks for no layout or frame there.

## 0.58.4 — 2026-11-08

Fixes:

- Opacity, `OffsetX` and `OffsetY` transitions run to their target (#90). They wrote the animated value into the node's own `opacity` and `offset`, the fields a transition reads its target from, so on the frame after a run started the transition took the value it had just drawn for a new target: with no render between frames the value stayed where it started, and with a render every frame it only crept toward the target (toasts and hover fades froze part way). Every animated property now goes through the animation overrides, and the node's own fields keep what the render set; layout, painting, hit testing and the devtools read the animated opacity and offset.
- A keyframe animation that fills forwards holds its pose on every later frame and after its component renders again, for every property (#90). Before, the pose held only for opacity and offsets; a colour, border, radius, size or transform went back to the node's own value the frame after the run ended.
- A forwards fill holds the pose the run stops on (#90): part way into an iteration for a fractional iteration count (`Count(1.5)` holds the half-way pose, in the direction that iteration plays), and the final pose for a zero duration, as in CSS. Before, a fractional count held the end of an iteration, and a zero-duration run held nothing.

Performance:

- A node that fades, or holds an opacity with fill forwards, keeps its layout cache and text layout across rebuilds (#90); only overrides of properties that shape layout make it lay out again. A logical wrapper holding full opacity that way is still flattened.

## 0.58.3 — 2026-11-07

Fixes:

- Frame pacing on DX12 with the wgpu engine (#88). wgpu waited for the swap chain's frame latency object inside `get_current_texture` while it held the device's fence lock, which `Queue::submit` needs, so on a FIFO swap chain no other thread could submit work on lurq's device until vblank. An app submitting GPU work from its own thread (a canvas painted on another thread) could submit only right after vblank, ahead of lurq's frame, which then missed the next vblank. The wgpu instance is now created with `Dx12UseFrameLatencyWaitableObject::DontWait`, and lurq waits on the same object itself right before `get_current_texture`, with no wgpu lock held and wgpu's 1000 ms timeout. Pacing and latency are unchanged for an app that submits only from the frame thread; the wait still counts in the render acquire phase of `perf_profile`. Other backends, other platforms and lurq's own DX12 renderer are not affected.

## 0.58.2 — 2026-11-06

Fixes:

- `VirtualizedList`: rows added or removed below the first row in view no longer anchor the list. Before, appending one row to a long list whose rows were not all measured yet (a chat opened at its end, then scrolled up one wheel tick) captured an anchor, measured every unmeasured row in chunks of 256 and held the offset in pixels until it finished, so the list froze for seconds and showed rows near its top meanwhile; a list at its end also laid out about 260 rows after every new row instead of its window. The row in view now stays where it was, and a removal that changes the height estimate shifts the offset by the estimate's change.

## 0.58.1 — 2026-11-05

Behaviour changes:

- A timer asks for a frame only when its callback changed what is shown (#85): a signal or store a component reads, an element ref, a scroll state, a text input or select state, a node-bound style, the theme, a canvas paint, or a focus or scroll-into-view request. Before, every timer that fired presented a frame, even with an empty callback, and on a FIFO swapchain a click or timer that came while that present waited for its vblank waited with it. A 12-step spinner beside an empty 25 ms interval now presents 19 frames in 0.6 s instead of 34, and handles 34 of 36 clicks and ticks at once instead of 14 of 33.

Tests:

- The frame-pacing test of input and timer latency runs on a virtual clock (#84), so it no longer flakes on a busy runner.

## 0.58.0 — 2026-11-05

Performance:

- Refreshing a component no longer copies its subtree into the slot caches. A slot keeps only what its own component rendered, with placeholders where its child components go, and the live tree takes the fresh output by move. Measured in Orchester (release build): opening the Orchestrator screen refreshes components in 34–36 ms instead of 76 ms, a long task conversation in 21–25 ms instead of 54–58 ms, the task list in 9–13 ms instead of 17–23 ms. What the layout after a refresh lays out again is unchanged, and pinned by tests.

Behaviour changes:

- Pointer input lands on what the next frame shows (#81). A press, a release or a wheel that comes before any frame laid out what an earlier event rebuilt (a hover that opened a box or pushed a row away) lays it out first. Before, it hit-tested the layout of the frame before: a wheel over a scroll box a hover had just opened was dropped, and a press where a hover had pushed a button away pressed that button.
- `VirtualizedList` keeps the first row in view where it is on screen when measured heights change (#82): a row that grows (the person opens it) or rows scrolled to that measure other than their estimate move the offset with the content above them, instead of moving everything in view. One tall row measured among few no longer moves the list by its skew of the average estimate. A list at its end stays at its end, and a scroll request (`set_scroll`, a pending scroll, a reveal) lands where it asked.
- `VirtualizedList` drops the measured height of a row whose key stays but whose item changed (another document under the same keys), which counts as the estimate until it is measured again. Before, such a row kept the height of the row it replaced, and the content could stay sized for the old document.

Added:

- `VirtualizedList::estimate_height(|item| ...)`: the height of a row the list has not measured yet, instead of the average of the rows it has; a list estimated this way is sized from its first pass without bootstrap rows.
- `LURQ_LAYOUT_TRACE` (with `perf_profile`): set to a file path, every layout pass appends one line per node it lays out without its cache, with the reason, and a summary line. See the profiling docs.
- `benches/component_refresh.rs` times component refreshes, and the layout after them.

## 0.57.0 — 2026-11-04

The layout performance rework that was `0.55.0-beta.1`, released with 0.56.0's changes; it takes 0.57.0 as 0.56.0 came out first. Layout results are meant to be exactly those of 0.56.0, and the public API is unchanged.

Performance (measured in one process against 0.54.0 and taffy 0.15.1 laying out the same trees, which all three place identically; Windows, release build):

- Laying out a tree where nothing changed costs about 35 ns, whatever its size, as taffy's does. In 0.54.0 it walked the whole tree and took 0.9 ms on 10,000 nodes and 0.5 ms on 5,461 nested ones. Dirt is now recorded where it happens (state styles, element handles, animations, scroll, text input) instead of being found by walking the tree, and the engine copies the theme only when it changed.
- Laying every node out again is 2 to 15 times faster: 10,000 rects in a column 3.2 ms → 1.6 ms (taffy 2.3 ms), 5,461 nested rows and columns 3.8 ms → 1.0 ms (taffy 10.3 ms), a chain of 64 containers 195 µs → 13 µs. A cached subtree is no longer copied when it is served, a laid-out parent no longer walks its subtree again, and flex lines allocate one `Vec` per container instead of six.
- A resize that leaves most of the tree's constraints alone lays out only what they reach: the 5,461 nested nodes at a new window width 622 µs → 0.36 µs (taffy 4.4 µs).
- A first layout is 1.4 to 3.3 times faster (nested 7.0 ms → 2.2 ms, taffy 10.1 ms; a column of 10,000 8.4 ms → 5.9 ms, taffy 5.9 ms), and building and rebuilding an element tree allocates almost nothing per node instead of two allocations: `set_root` takes over the old tree's layout caches and watches instead of cloning them, a node's layout watch is made when something first reads it, and an element reuses the box its predecessor was moved out of.
- A 1 MiB stack, the size of the Windows main thread, lays out 377 nested containers in a release build (102 in 0.51.2): rare paths of leaf and scroll layout and the child-adding steps of `Row::with` and `Column::with` no longer sit in the frames that stay on the stack across the recursion.

Behaviour changes:

- None intended. Worth reporting: a layout that does not update after a change (a hover, focus or active style that changes size or padding, an element handle mutation, an animation, scrolling, text input); runtime state lost across a component's render (scroll offsets, text input state including styled spans, focus, element ids); a stack overflow on a deep tree.

Added:

- `benches/vs_taffy.rs` lays out the same 12 trees with lurq and taffy in six modes (build, cold, rebuild, forced, resize, noop), after checking that both place every leaf alike, and probes the deepest tree a given stack lays out (`VS_TAFFY_PROBE`). `benches/vs_taffy_allocs.rs` counts the allocations of each mode. The `layout` and `render_list` benches lay out now; before they timed only building the tree. `scripts/check.ps1` runs the test suite and the release-only stack-depth test.

## 0.56.0 — 2026-11-04

Added:

- Middle-button autoscroll, on per scroll container (#78). `middle_button_autoscroll(true)` on `ScrollVertical`, `ScrollHorizontal`, `ScrollBoth` or `VirtualizedList` makes a middle press over the container mark where the button went down. While the button is held, the content scrolls toward the pointer on the axes the container scrolls, faster the farther the pointer is from the mark, and does not move within 12 logical pixels of it. The release, Esc, another press or the pointer leaving the window ends it, and the release is not a middle click. The innermost container with it on that can scroll takes the press, unless an element inside takes the middle button itself: one with a middle-click handler, a middle drag, or `prevent_default` on the press. The container's scroll handlers get the start, steps and end of the scroll, and its scroll-reach handlers run as they do for the wheel. It is off by default. `LayoutKind::ScrollModifier` has a new `middle_button_autoscroll` field.

0.55.0 is left for the layout-performance line, whose `0.55.0-beta.1` is on crates.io.

## 0.55.0-beta.1 — 2026-11-02

A beta of the layout performance rework released as 0.57.0; its changes are listed there.

## 0.54.0 — 2026-11-02

Added:

- Fills behind ranges of a text, such as the matches of a search (#75). `Text::highlights` takes `TextHighlight` values (a UTF-8 byte range, a color or palette role and a corner radius, `TextHighlight::new(range, color).radius(r)`) and draws one fill per laid-out line each range covers, from the carets of the painted layout, as a selection is drawn: under the glyphs and under a selection, in each highlight's own color. The fills follow the text when it wraps again, and the text is shaped as without them; a range is cut at the text's end, its ends inside a character move back to its start, and an empty range draws nothing. A text that is not selectable now measures its carets when it has highlights. With the `markdown` feature, `MarkdownProps::highlights` does the same over a document, with ranges in bytes of its rendered text, which `MarkdownProps::rendered_text` returns for the app to search: the text of its paragraphs, headings, list items, table cells and code blocks in document order, one line feed between two of them, without list markers, footnote labels, a code block's language or a link's isolate marks. A range may cover several of them; inside a link the fill leaves the link's isolate marks out, as a selection does.

Fixes:

- With the `devtools` feature, a `Signal<SelectionStyle>` or a `Signal<HashMap<..>>` did not compile, as neither implemented `DevtoolsInspectable`. Both do now; a map shows its length, as a `HashSet` does.

## 0.53.0 — 2026-10-29

Added:

- Trackpad swipes between pages, as browsers go back and forward. `on_history_swipe` (on `Element` builders and every component) gets a `HistorySwipeEvent` with a `direction` (`HistoryDirection::Back` for fingers to the right, `Forward` to the left) when two fingers that moved across the window sideways lift: far enough (120 logical pixels), mostly sideways (twice as far as up or down), and without a scroll container moving with them or a scroll handler preventing their default, so horizontal scrolling is unchanged and a sideways list at its edge lets the next swipe through. The event goes to the elements under the pointer, innermost first, until one stops its propagation. The momentum after a swipe is never a second one, and a swipe that interrupts that momentum still counts. Mouse wheels (scrolls without phases) and lurq's synthetic input make none. On macOS the swipes follow System Settings › Trackpad › "Swipe between pages" with two fingers (`NSEvent.isSwipeTrackingFromScrollEventsEnabled`, read as each gesture starts); set to three fingers only, AppKit sends a swipe event winit does not report, and no swipe is recognized. Other platforms report no scroll phases and have no swipes yet. lurq's `Router` does not follow swipes by itself: call `RouterHandle::back` / `forward` from `on_history_swipe`. `Tree::set_history_swipes` (hidden) turns the recognizer on in tests.

## 0.52.1 — 2026-10-29

Fixes:

- A screenshot of a macOS window nobody could see never completed. While the window is occluded (its application hidden, the window minimized or fully covered) Metal hands out no drawable and wgpu's `get_current_texture` answers `Occluded`, so the frame the capture waited for was skipped, and the next frame without a capture failed it as superseded: `WindowHandle::screenshot` wrote nothing and MCP `lurq_screenshot` answered "superseded before a frame was rendered". When the surface gives no texture and a capture waits, the wgpu renderer now draws that frame into a texture of the surface's size and format, takes the capture from it and presents nothing. Frames without a capture are still skipped while the window cannot be seen.

## 0.52.0 — 2026-10-28

Added:

- Styled text inputs, with the `markdown` feature. `TextInput::spans(Signal<Vec<StyledSpan>>)` makes an input edit bold, italic, inline code, strikethrough and links over its plain `Signal<String>` value, as UTF-8 byte ranges the app owns: the input draws the runs with the new theme roles `ThemeTextMarks` (`Theme::text_marks`, `set_text_marks`), moves them with every edit (typing, deleting, cut, paste, input method composition), keeps them in the same undo history as the text and writes them back. Typed text takes the style of the text before the caret, except that a link does not grow past its end. `TextInputController` gains `toggle_mark`, `set_mark`, `set_link`, `set_pending_style` (the style of the next typed text, for Ctrl+B while typing), `selection_style` (all, some or none per mark, for a toolbar), `styled_text`, `selected_styled_text` and `replace_selection_styled` (a paste handler's parsed spans). `TextInput::on_styled_change` reports every change the input makes as a `StyledChange` (the `StyledEdit`, the spans shared rather than copied, and the text on demand), `copy_format` sets what Ctrl+C copies from the styled selection (such as Markdown), and `on_link_click` reports a link clicked with Ctrl (Cmd on macOS). `TextInputHandle::copied_text` tells what Ctrl+C would copy. See the *Styled text inputs* guide and `examples/styled_editor.rs`. Plain inputs are unchanged.

Performance:

- A decorated input (input tokens, styled spans) without padded tokens no longer builds the cluster index padded rows need, about 40% less layout time for a long paragraph.
- A wrapped decorated input lays out a paragraph at a time and keeps each paragraph's layout, so an edit lays out only the paragraph it changed. Justified text and single-line inputs still lay out whole.
- A long left-to-right paragraph of a decorated input is shaped in chunks cut after spaces at places its own words choose; each chunk is kept, so an edit shapes only the chunk it changed. The paragraph lays out exactly as when shaped whole. Paragraphs with right-to-left text, bidi controls or tabs are shaped whole.
- A decorated input keeps its glyph commands between frames: moving the caret, selecting and scrolling no longer place every glyph again. Its rows find their clusters with sorted lists instead of ordered maps.

## 0.51.2 — 2026-10-26

Fixes:

- What the code that mounts a component puts on the element `mount` returns was lost when that component rendered again by itself. `ctx.mount::<C>(props).id("x")` (or `Markdown::mount(ctx, props).id("x")`) put the id on the component's root in the parent's rendered tree only; a dirty refresh of just that component (its own signal, or a theme or breakpoint change it reads) swapped its fresh render into the window without it, so `Tree::get_element_by_id` and MCP `lurq_find_by_id` no longer found the component until the parent rendered again. The same happened to a `.key(..)`, `.class(..)` or `.classes(..)` put there, and to what a `Draggable` (drag handlers, element ref, payload) or a `DropZone` (drop handler) puts on a child component's root: dragging such a component, or dropping on it, stopped working after it rendered by itself. A component's root now records what was put on it after `mount` returned, and its fresh root takes that over wherever it replaces the old one: in the window's tree and in the kept trees of the clean components above it. The outer id and key win over the component's own, as when the parent renders; outer classes are added to the component's own. The parent does not render again for this, and a parent that renders again without the decoration drops it. Kept: those four `Element` methods and what `Draggable` and `DropZone` put on their child, which is all that can be put on a mounted component's root from outside lurq. Not covered, as it is not put on the component's root: what a `DragContainer` puts on the draggables nested in its child (it rewraps their drag start to keep them in its bounds) and the `selectable` flag a selectable `Markdown` sets on the nodes of the components it mounts.

## 0.51.1 — 2026-10-23

Fixes:

- On Windows, a precision touchpad's slanted swipe still nudged a code block (or any `ScrollHorizontal`) under the pointer sideways while the page scrolled. winit delivers `WM_MOUSEWHEEL` and `WM_MOUSEHWHEEL` as separate events, one with a vertical delta and one with a horizontal, so 0.51.0's rule, which compares the two deltas of one event, never saw a slant. A horizontal-only delta is now weighed against the vertical delta seen within the last 150 ms at the same pointer (within 8 logical pixels): while that vertical delta dominates, a `ScrollHorizontal` takes none of it and the event goes to the containers around it. A mouse's Shift+wheel, with no vertical wheel just before it at that spot, a deliberate sideways swipe, and a horizontal burst after the window has passed scroll the block as before; macOS, which sends both deltas in one event, is unchanged. `Tree::set_animation_clock` (hidden, for tests) now also sets the clock this window is read on.
- A Markdown code block's box and scroll node were keyed by the hash of the block's first line, so a one-line block that was still streaming changed its key with every token: its scroll reset, a selection in it was dropped, and its node ids and MCP refs churned. They are now keyed by the block's ordinal among the document's code blocks and its language, which hold while the block streams and while paragraphs are added or removed around it. A code block inserted above a scrolled block, or removed from above it, now shifts the ordinals, and the blocks after it take the scroll of the block that had their ordinal (0.51.0 kept each block's scroll in that case, by its first line).
- MCP `scroll_to` on an element larger than its scroll container's viewport left it where it was when its start was inside the view by less than the 8 px margin. The start is now brought at least the margin inside the view; an element whose start already is stays put. (This and 0.51.0's change, which shows such an element's start instead of its end, are the only behaviour changes to `scroll_to`.)
- The headings of the 0.50.0 and 0.49.1 entries below, which the 0.51.0 release renamed to 0.51.0 by mistake, read their own versions again.

## 0.51.0 — 2026-10-23

Behaviour changes:

- A Markdown code block whose lines are wider than its box scrolls sideways inside it. Its lines stay unwrapped, as written; the box, its language label and the document keep their width, so the block never makes the document wider; and a horizontal scrollbar shows over the box's bottom padding while the block overflows, as lurq's other scroll containers show theirs. Shift+wheel and a trackpad's sideways swipe scroll it, from the innermost scroll container outward as any scroll does; a plain vertical wheel over the block scrolls the page or list around it, not the block, so a chat's follow-to-end is not caught by a block that only overflows sideways. A block whose lines fit shows no bar and lays out exactly as before, in a filling and in a fitted (`fit_content`) document, nested in a list or a quote too. Selection and copy cross a scrolled block as any block, the lines out of view included, and the block's scroll is kept when the document renders again (a streaming reply keeps its place). Before, the lines were laid out at the box's inner width and a long line was painted past it and clipped, out of reach. A math block (`$$`) is a code block and scrolls the same way. Each block's box and scroll node are keyed by the first line of its code, so a block keeps its scroll when the document renders again with a block inserted above it, a block removed, a label added or a reply streaming on (unkeyed nodes are paired by position). As with every scroll container, a Shift+wheel over a block that is at its start edge (or its end, for the other direction) is consumed by nothing and no longer reaches `on_scroll` handlers on the containers around it; and an MCP client sees each code block as an interactive scroll node in the tree, between the box and its lines.
- A scroll container that scrolls sideways only (`ScrollHorizontal`) takes no part of a mostly vertical gesture: when a wheel or trackpad delta has `|dx| < |dy|`, the container leaves the whole delta to the containers around it instead of taking its `dx`, so a chat's slightly slanted swipe scrolls the chat and nudges no code block under the pointer. A mostly sideways swipe and Shift+wheel (a delta on x alone) scroll it as before, and a mostly sideways swipe's `dy` still goes to the container around it. `ScrollVertical` and `ScrollBoth` are unchanged.
- Breaking for struct literals: `ThemeMarkdown` gains the field `code_block_scrollbar` (`ThemeMarkdown { .., ..ThemeMarkdown::default() }` is unchanged). A code block's box is now a column without padding around a padded label and a padded horizontal scroll container of the lines; the padding, the words' places and the box's size are the same, but an element lookup that counted on the lines being the box's direct child sees the scroll container between them.

Fixes:

- MCP `scroll_to` on an element larger than its scroll container's viewport (a code block's line wider than its box) scrolled the container to the element's end. It now shows the element's start, as `scroll_into_view` does, and leaves an element whose start is already in view where it is.

Added:

- `ThemeMarkdown::code_block_scrollbar: Option<ScrollBarStyle>`: the horizontal scrollbar of a code block that overflows, drawn over the box's bottom padding. The default is a thin light bar (`ScrollBarStyle::thin()` with a `#94a3b8` thumb at half opacity, inset 4 px from the bottom and 6 px from the corners) for the default dark box; `None` draws the app theme's scrollbar, always as an overlay: a `Reserved` placement is not reserved in a code block, so a block whose lines fit keeps its size.
- `ScrollBarStyle`, `ScrollBarVisibility` and `ScrollBarPlacement` implement `Debug` and `PartialEq`, so a theme holding a bar compares.

## 0.50.0 — 2026-10-22

Behaviour changes:

- A `VirtualizedList` bootstraps from where it opens. Before it has measured a row it cannot size its window (the viewport is not laid out yet), so its first pass renders a fixed number of bootstrap rows whose heights seed the estimate the next pass sizes the window with. It rendered its first 64 rows, from the top, whatever the list was asked to open on, so a chat opened at its end (`scroll_to_bottom_pending` on its `ScrollState`) built and laid out its 64 oldest turns before rendering its newest, and did so again whenever another conversation was swapped in under it (no key survives, so the heights were cleared). It now renders 16 rows (`.bootstrap_rows(n)` sets another number), taken from the end when a scroll to the bottom is queued, from the `reveal_key`'s row when there is one, or else from the top. A list opened at its end builds its last 16 rows and none from its top, the same work for 300 rows and for 3,000; its first rows are built when they are scrolled to. When those rows are shorter than the viewport, the queued scroll is kept until the window is sized from their measured heights, so the list still lands at its end. A list opened at its top renders its first rows, as before, 16 instead of 64: a list of short rows fills its viewport one frame later than it did, and passes a larger `bootstrap_rows` if that frame matters. A list opened at an offset (a queued `set_scroll_pending`, or the offset a scroll state shared with the list before still holds) renders its first rows too, since no height names the row at that offset yet, and is scrolled to the offset once they are measured, again while the content sized from the estimate grows past it as the rows at its end are measured; it used to be clamped to the bootstrap rows and, as that was their end, follow to the real end. A scroll of the person's in between stands, and items swapped in with all-new keys drop the offset the list was still restoring for the rows before (one the app queued for the new rows stays). When the items are swapped for others whose keys all differ, the measured heights go but their average stays as the estimate, so the new rows are windowed where the list opens, about a viewport's worth, instead of bootstrapped from the top again.
- A context provided in `render` renders again only the descendants that read it. A component renders again for a context when a value of a type it read (`use_context`, `consume_context`, in `create`, a render or a handler) is provided anew, or when its own render provides values, since only a render provides them again; every other component keeps its rendered tree and passes the contexts on, so a reader below one renders in place with the pass and sees the new value; a `for_each` item that read the value (or provides) is rendered by its owner, whose render holds its closure. A component providing values in its render used to render every descendant again, read or not. `Tree::get_element_by_id`, `get_element_by_id_mut`, `get_elements_by_class_name` and the MCP element tree flush such readers too, refreshing again while a refresh replaced something (up to the eight refreshes a pass makes), so a lookup between passes sees the settled subtrees. `provide` still compares nothing: each call is a new providing, and the readers of that type render again; `Ctx::provide_if_changed` keeps the earlier providing while the value is equal (`PartialEq`), so nothing renders again for it.

Fixes:

- A `Markdown` document with a link in it kept the window's layout dirty: every idle pass found it dirty and laid the window out again, for as long as the document was shown (a chat showing a reply with a link relaid its window on every frame, ~2–3 ms in release). A link's node is placed by its paragraph's layout and holds no layout of its own, so the marks that ask for its layout (its anchor's attachment, a hover or focus style) were never consumed; the paragraph's layout now consumes them. A document without a link settled as before, and one with a link settles with it; a hovered link whose style keeps its metrics is painted, not laid out again.

Added:

- `VirtualizedList::bootstrap_rows(n)`: how many rows the list renders before it has measured one (16 by default).
- `Ctx::provide_if_changed(value)`: `provide`, unless the value equals the one this component provided before (`PartialEq`), in which case the descendants that read it see the same providing and do not render again. In `render` the value is compared with the previous render's; in `create` or a handler, with the one provided there.

## 0.49.1 — 2026-10-21

Behaviour changes:

- Hover follows content that moves under a pointer that holds still. After every layout that changed (a wheel scroll, a list that grew, a stick-to-bottom chat taking a message, any relayout), lurq hit-tests the hover again at the pointer's last position, like a browser: `on_mouse_enter`, `on_mouse_leave`, `hovered` styles and the cursor now change without a mouse event, in the frame that shows the new layout, and the winit shell applies the cursor after each frame. The handlers run with the source of the input that last moved the pointer, so an agent's pointer stays synthetic input. Nothing is hovered while the pointer is outside the window or a drag holds it, and a click held back for a double click no longer re-hovers what was under it after the pointer moved on or left.
- A hover style that moves its own node away from a still pointer (a `hovered` size, offset or transform, with or without a transition) unhovers the node, which moves it back under the pointer, which hovers it again: like CSS `:hover`, such a node flickers, now also while the pointer holds still. Keep hover styles from moving the node off the pointer.
- An anchored overlay (`Overlay`, `Popup`, a Markdown link's tooltip), a `Select`'s menu and a `Modal` over an element are not drawn while their anchor (the select, the target element) is scrolled or clipped wholly out of view, and come back with it. Their open state is kept, and such a hidden popup is dismissed like a CSS popover that is out of view: a left press anywhere closes it (with `dismiss_on_outside_click`) and is not consumed, so it also reaches what it lands on; Escape closes it (with `dismiss_on_escape`); and keyboard focus inside it goes back to its anchor, or the first focusable element inside the anchor, or else the nearest focusable ancestor of the anchor. A tooltip with no open signal just stays hidden.

Fixes:

- A tooltip shown on hover stayed on screen when the content scrolled under a pointer that held still, and was drawn over the header above the list, away from its link, which had scrolled under that header (a Markdown link's tooltip in a chat). Hover was hit-tested only on mouse events, so the link never got its `on_mouse_leave`. See the first behaviour change.
- An anchored overlay (a Markdown link's tooltip shown for keyboard focus, an open `Popup`) and a `Select`'s menu followed their anchor out of a scroll container's viewport and were drawn over whatever covered the anchor, such as the header above a list. See the third behaviour change.

## 0.49.0 — 2026-10-20

Behaviour changes:

- A paste shortcut delivered as synthetic input (an MCP client's `lurq_interact` `key` of `Ctrl+V`, `Cmd+V` or `Shift+Insert`, an applied `SyntheticInput::key_down`) no longer reads the OS clipboard: the person's clipboard is theirs, and an agent driving the window must not paste from it silently. The shortcut is refused and inserts nothing. A synthetic paste carries its own content instead: `SyntheticInput::paste(content)`, or the MCP `paste` action with `text` and/or `files`. A person's `Ctrl+V` pastes as before.
- An `Image` node's pixels are placed by its `fit`: `Stretch` by default, which draws as every image drew, so nothing changes unless `Image::fit`, `contain()` or `cover()` is called.
- Breaking for exhaustive matches and struct literals: `SyntheticInputKind` gains the variants `Paste`, `FileDragEnter`, `FileDragOver`, `FileDragLeave` and `FileDrop`, and `EventHandlers` the fields `on_file_drag_enter`, `on_file_drag_over`, `on_file_drag_leave`, `on_file_drop` and `on_paste`. Every other existing API is unchanged.

Added:

- Clipboard images and files (feature `clipboard`): `lurq::clipboard::read_clipboard() -> Option<ClipboardContent>` reads everything the clipboard holds at once, the text, the image a screenshot tool or a browser put there (Windows `CF_DIBV5`/`CF_DIB`, macOS `public.png`/`public.tiff`, read through `arboard` as RGBA) and the files copied in Explorer or Finder (`CF_HDROP`, file URLs); a clipboard may hold text and an image together, and `ClipboardContent::kinds()` tells which. `ClipboardImage::to_png()` encodes an image to store it (through the `png` crate, which `image` already pulled in); `ClipboardImage::from_rgba(width, height, rgba)` checks the size and bytes of one built by hand. `copy_image_to_clipboard(width, height, &rgba)` and `copy_files_to_clipboard(&paths)` write the other way. `ClipboardContent`, `ClipboardImage`, `ClipboardKinds` and `ClipboardImageError` live in `lurq::app::events` and are built without the feature, so a headless test pastes without an OS clipboard; the text functions are unchanged.
- A paste event on text inputs: `on_paste(|PasteEvent| …)`, on the input or on an ancestor it bubbles to, runs before the default inserts the text. `Ctrl+V` (`Cmd+V` on macOS), `Shift+Insert`, `WindowHandle::paste_from_clipboard()` (for an app's own Paste menu item or context menu; feature `clipboard`) and `Tree::paste(content)` all go through it. The event carries the `ClipboardContent` (`text()`, `image()`, `files()`, `kinds()`, `content()`), its `target_id` and the `input_id`; `prevent_default()` keeps the text out of the input, `stop_propagation()` ends the bubbling. So an app takes an image or files and stops the insertion, turns a long text into an attachment, or lets plain text paste as it always did. The handler runs inside the input's delivery, so `is_synthetic_input()` tells an agent's paste from a person's. The clipboard is read as far as the paste needs: a paste no `on_paste` handler looks at reads only the text, as before; one a handler looks at reads the image and the files too. A `paste_from_clipboard()` request made while lurq delivers synthetic input (an MCP client's click on the app's Paste menu item) is refused by the shell (`Tree::paste_from_os_clipboard(synthetic)`), like a synthetic shortcut.
- Files dragged into the window from the OS (Explorer, Finder) reach the element under the pointer, with overlays and modals hit-tested as for the mouse: `on_file_drag_enter(FileDragEvent)` once when the drag comes over the element, `on_file_drag_over(FileDragEvent)` on every move over it (`x`, `y` in logical pixels), `on_file_drag_leave()` when it moves off or leaves the window, and `on_file_drop(FileDropEvent)` on the drop; enter, over and drop bubble to the ancestors. `event.paths` (`DraggedPaths`) is given once for the whole drag, however many files it holds, and reports folders as folders (`files()`, `folders()`, `has_folders()`, `DroppedPath::is_folder()`), looked up once; text or an image dragged from a browser is ignored, since winit reports nothing for it. A `drag_over` state style (`.drag_over(|style| …)`, `drag_over_style`, `InteractionState::is_drag_over()`) applies while the drag is over the element, like `hovered`. `event.refuse()` on an enter or over event refuses the drag at the element whose handler called it, not at its ancestors or siblings, for as long as the drag stays over it: no element shows the style and `Tree::file_drag_accepted()` reports `Some(false)`, and leaving and entering the element again asks again; the OS cursor is not changed, since winit answers the OS with a copy effect before lurq sees the drag, and the drop is still delivered to `on_file_drop`, whose handler ignores what it refused. A shell drives it with `Tree::file_drag_enter(paths, x, y)`, `file_drag_over(x, y)`, `file_drag_leave()` and `file_drop(paths, x, y)`; the winit shell collects the OS's burst of one event per file into one session on Windows and macOS and reads the pointer from the OS every 16 ms while the drag hovers (`GetCursorPos`, `NSEvent::mouseLocation`), delivering `on_file_drag_over` when it moved, because the OS owns the pointer during a drag and winit reports no position; a hover ends as a leave when no mouse button has been pressed for 250 ms of polls with no file event in between (the OS drag ended without winit telling; `GetAsyncKeyState` on Windows, `NSEvent::pressedMouseButtons` on macOS; the grace period covers the release, which the OS reports before its drop callback, so a drop within it continues the session without a leave) or when it neither moved nor got a drop or a cancel for 60 s, since its source is gone and polling would never stop. On Linux the last pointer position the window saw is used. Secondary windows take drops too.
- Images decoded at runtime (feature `image`): `Image::from_bytes(bytes)`, `Image::from_file(path)` and `Image::from_source(source, key)` build a node that worker threads decode off the UI thread (PNG, JPEG, the first frame of a GIF or WebP, BMP, TIFF). The node shows its `.placeholder(element)` and its own background until the pixels are there, then the image, and the `.error_view(|error| …)` built from the `ImageDecodeError` for a broken file, never a panic. `.downscale_to(width, height)` caps the decoded size, keeping the aspect ratio and never upscaling, so a thumbnail of a 4K screenshot does not keep its 30 MB of RGBA. `.fit(BackgroundSize)`, `.contain()`, `.cover()` and `.stretch()` place the pixels in the box of any image node. Decoded pixels are kept in the process-wide cache `lurq::images::decoded_images()` (`DecodedImages`), keyed by content and size cap: `from_bytes` hashes the bytes (`ImageKey::of_bytes`), `from_file` keys the path with the file's size and modification time (`ImageKey::of_path`), and `Image::from_source(ImageSource::Bytes(arc), ImageKey::new(hash))` takes a hash the app stores without hashing or copying the bytes (`.cache_key` replaces the key after `from_bytes` has hashed). A chat laid out again, or a `VirtualizedList` row built again as it scrolls, finds its image ready in the first pass and decodes nothing. The cache keeps at most `budget()` bytes of RGBA (`DEFAULT_BUDGET_BYTES`, 128 MiB; `set_budget`), dropping the least recently used ready images; an image larger than the whole budget on its own, decoded or inserted, is still shown and leaves the cache at the next eviction while the node showing it keeps its pixels; `state(key, cap)` reads an entry, `insert` puts pixels the app decoded, `wait_idle(timeout)` and `lurq::images::wait_for_decoded_images(timeout)` block until no decode is pending, `DecodedImages::new()` makes a cache of a test's own, and `decode_image(&source, cap)` decodes on the calling thread. The decode's end wakes the window's loop, so the image is drawn without the app doing anything.
- Headless helpers: `SyntheticInput::paste(content)`, `file_drag_enter(paths, x, y)`, `file_drag_over(x, y)`, `file_drag_leave()` and `file_drop(paths, x, y)` reach the same entry points as the shell, marked synthetic; `Tree::set_clipboard_reader(Some(Arc<dyn ClipboardReader>))` installs a clipboard of a test's own for a person's `Ctrl+V` (`lurq::app::ClipboardReader`: `read_text()` for a paste no handler looks at, `read()` for one an `on_paste` handler looks at), so a test never touches the OS clipboard; `DraggedPaths::from_paths` and `DraggedPaths::new([DroppedPath::with_kind(path, is_folder)])` build the paths of a drag, and `ClipboardContent::text`, `image`, `files` with `with_text`, `with_image`, `with_files` the content of a paste.
- MCP `lurq_interact` actions: `paste` (`text` and/or `files`, delivered to the focused text input and its ancestors' `on_paste` handlers), `drag_files` and `drop_files` (`paths` over or onto a `ref` or point, as an Explorer or Finder drag), and `file_drag_leave`.
- `Tree::has_file_drag()`, `Tree::file_drag_paths()` and `Tree::file_drag_accepted()` report the drag over the window.

## 0.48.2 — 2026-10-20

Fixes:

- A single-line text with an ellipsis (`TextOverflow::Elipsis`, `nowrap`) and a shrink factor in a Row or Column beside a growing child (a `Spacer::flex(1.0)`) was cut although its words fit, with the free room still beside it: "Pausing run #4 of KON-10 · dismiss…" in a box 1/16000 px narrower than its words. The line granted the growing child the room left and then summed its children back up to find the overflow to shrink by; in `f32` the sum could come out a few micro-pixels over the line, and the text was laid out again that much narrower and ellipsized, since a text over its box by any amount is cut. Whether it happened depended on the fractions of the line's width and of its texts' widths (a time in a label, a title's words), so most titles of a kind were fine and some were cut at some window widths. The overflow of a line now counts the free room its growing children fill once, as it was granted: the children sized on their own, a flex basis, and what a child takes beyond its grant are what can overflow the line. A row too narrow for its words still ellipsizes them.

## 0.48.1 — 2026-10-17

Fixes:

- Inline code and code blocks in Markdown are drawn in a monospaced font. The default Markdown theme, `ThemeFonts::mono` and the devtools ask for the family `"monospace"`, and a family name was always looked up as a font of that name: there is none, so that text was drawn in the fallback font, proportional (Segoe UI on Windows, DejaVu Sans or Lato on Linux). A `font_family` that is a CSS generic family (`"serif"`, `"sans-serif"`, `"monospace"`, `"cursive"`, `"fantasy"`, in any ASCII case) now asks for the platform's font for it (Consolas on Windows, Menlo on macOS, DejaVu Sans Mono on Linux for `monospace`; `GenericFamily` lists them all), in plain text, rich text and Markdown spans, text inputs and canvas text, for measuring, painting, carets and selection alike. `sans-serif` draws as the empty family does. The fonts are chosen when the `App` is created; to draw a generic family in a bundled font, load it and register an alias of the keyword (`app.register_font("monospace", "JetBrains Mono")`), which is still resolved first, and any other name is still a font's. With the default Markdown theme, table header cells, table cells, table markers and the code block's language label are styled from inline code too, so they become monospaced as well. Code and table text is wider than before, so Markdown with code or tables wraps and measures differently.
- A font alias registered for a generic family's keyword applies to the keyword in any ASCII case: `register_font("monospace", …)` also serves `"Monospace"`, as the generic family itself does.

Added:

- `lurq::layout::text_style::GenericFamily`, with `GenericFamily::from_name` (the generic family a font family name names, if any) and `GenericFamily::keyword`.

## 0.48.0 — 2026-10-16

Fixes:

- Selecting text follows the glyphs on screen. A selectable Markdown paragraph with spans in other fonts or sizes (inline code, a heading's code, emphasis in another face) had its caret positions measured from the paragraph's text in its first span's style alone, while the paragraph was drawn span by span: wherever a span was wider or narrower in the first style, and wherever the lines wrapped elsewhere, the caret under the pointer, the highlight and the copied text indexed another layout than the one drawn. A drag over a chat reply from "The design marks" to "planned" copied from "ttachments" to the end of the paragraph, the highlight of a line ran past its last glyph to the paragraph's edge, and below the last line of the layout the carets came from every point mapped to the end of the text, so no selection could be started or extended there. Caret positions now come from the layout the text is painted with: span by span, in device pixels at the display scale, wrapped at the box's width rounded up in device pixels (`ceil(width * scale)`), and brought back to logical pixels. That is also the layout the paint finds in the glyph engine's caches; a logical layout at a fractional width or scale (1.25, 1.5) is not it, so plain `Text` is measured the same way. Hit-testing, the highlight and the copied text now index one layout: the drawn one.
- A selectable `Text` or Markdown paragraph in a box taller than its text (a `Text` filling a window or a fixed-height box, a paragraph stretched by a row; `VerticalAlign::Center` is the default) hit-tested and highlighted from the top of its box while its glyphs were drawn centered in it: a press on the drawn first line could select from the last. Layout now records where the text's first line sits in its box, as the paint places it, and hit-testing and the highlight read that. A `Text`'s padding does not move its glyphs, and does not move its selection either.
- A right-to-left glyph's carets were mirrored: the caret before its cluster was put at its left edge and the caret after it at its right, so a drag over Hebrew or Arabic text from the right edge of its first letter lost that letter and a drag to the left edge of its last letter lost that one. Plain text, Markdown and text inputs alike.
- A caret or a selection that ends inside a ligature is drawn there. A font may draw several characters as one glyph (DejaVu Sans, the usual fallback font on Linux, draws the "fi" of "first" as one; programming fonts draw "--" or "=>" as one), and caret positions were kept only at the edges of glyphs: in a text input, the caret between the "f" and the "i" was drawn at the end of the text, a selection that ended there was highlighted to the end of the row, and a press on the glyph could only land before or after both characters. A ligature's advance is now split evenly among its graphemes, as cosmic-text's own hit-testing splits it, in plain text, Markdown and text inputs. A text input with styled tokens (`TextInput::tokens`) still keeps its carets at the edges of the ligature.
- A selection highlight in a `Text` or Markdown paragraph no longer covers the whitespace and invisible marks at the end of a wrapped row (a space before a link that moved to the next line, with the isolate mark lurq puts before the link): the text layout lets them hang past the row and draws nothing there. A row that ends with a line break or with the text keeps its trailing spaces highlighted. A highlight covers the advances of the selected glyphs, from the caret before the first to the caret after the last; there is no marker for a line break. A text input keeps every selected character visible, a hanging space at a wrapped row too.
- `VirtualizedList::reveal_key` of a row taller than the viewport no longer holds the list at that row. Such a row is never fully visible, so its reveal stayed pending and every layout scrolled the row's top back to the viewport's top: the wheel and the scrollbar moved the list for one frame and it jumped back, until the key changed. The reveal now ends once the row's top is at the viewport's top. A chat that reveals its newest message on opening could not be scrolled while that message was taller than the view.
- A left click that lurq holds back because a node under it has an `on_dblclick` handler keeps the source of its input. A pass or the next click delivers it later, outside that input's delivery, so its handlers (`on_click`, and a Markdown link's `on_link_activate` and `on_link_click`) saw `is_synthetic_input()` as `false` for an MCP client's click or an applied `SyntheticInput`: an app that refuses synthetic input on a link or control acted on it as on a person's click. A person's click held back this way and delivered by an MCP client's next click was marked synthetic; it is OS input now.

Behaviour changes:

- A Shift press on a selectable text extends the selection from where it started (the pressed point of the last plain press, or the start of a double- or triple-click's word or line) to the pressed point, across texts too, as in a browser; a drag with Shift held goes on from there. Shift used to start a new selection in the pressed text while keeping the other texts' selections; that is what Ctrl still does. Without a selection to extend, a Shift press starts one at the pressed point. Escape, a press on nothing selectable and a press in a text input forget where the selection started.
- The click that a key makes on a focused control (Enter on a Markdown link, Enter or Space on a focused button) carries the key's Shift, Ctrl, Alt and Meta, and `MouseEvent::from_keyboard` marks it. Its modifiers were always `false`, so a handler could not tell Ctrl+Enter from Enter.
- A `Meta` or `Super` key that an MCP client holds (`lurq_interact` `key_down`) sets `meta` on that window's later pointer events too (moves, presses, releases, clicks), as it does on key events. Pointer events had no `meta`, so a held Meta reached key events only.

Added:

- `Tree::selected_text()`: the text selected in the selectable texts as Ctrl+C copies it (one line per visual line, presentation such as a link's isolate marks and trailing runs left out), or `None`. An app copies or shows the selection without the `clipboard` feature, and a test checks what a drag selects.
- `TextInputHandle::selected_text()`: the selected part of a text input's value.
- `MarkdownProps::on_link_activate(f)`: called with the link and the `MouseEvent` that activated it (a left click, a middle click, or Enter on the focused link), so an app can follow browser rules: a middle click or Ctrl+click (Cmd+click on macOS) opens the link in a background tab, Ctrl+Shift+click opens it and switches to it. The event's `button` is `Left` or `Middle` (`Left` for Enter), `from_keyboard` is set for Enter, and `shift`, `ctrl`, `alt` and `meta` are the modifiers held. A middle click is a press and a release of the middle button on the same link, like a left click: a press released off the link activates nothing, and neither does a press on one link released on another more than a few pixels away (closer, the link under the release activates, as a left click always did). The press starts no text selection and no drag of what the link is in. `on_link_click` is unchanged (left clicks and Enter) and runs after `on_link_activate` when both are set; with `on_link_activate` set, a link to an in-app path no longer navigates the router by itself. The callback runs inside the input's dispatch, so `is_synthetic_input()` tells an MCP client's `lurq_interact` click (`"button": "middle"`, `"modifiers": [...]`) from a person's.
- `MouseEvent::meta`, on every mouse event (move, down, up, click, double-click): Cmd on macOS, the Windows key on Windows, Super elsewhere. The winit shell fills it from `ModifiersState::super_key`, and synthetic input from `SyntheticModifiers::meta` (MCP `"modifiers": ["meta"]`), which pointer events used to drop. A shell that drives a `Tree` itself passes it with `Tree::mouse_move_with_meta`, `mouse_down_with_meta` and `mouse_up_with_meta`; `mouse_*_with_modifiers` report it released.
- `MouseEvent::from_keyboard`: a key made this click, not the pointer (Enter on a focused link, Enter or Space on a focused button). Its `button` is `Left` and `x`, `y` are 0, as for a browser's keyboard click.
- `Tree::register_keyframes_in_phase` (and `AnimationEngine::register_keyframes_in_phase`): every run of these keyframes plays on the process's animation clock, starting at the last whole number of its periods since the clock's origin (`lurq::animation::clock_origin()`) instead of when its node first ticks. The period is the duration, or twice it for `Alternate` and `AlternateReverse`, so every run shows the same pose in every direction. Spinners that mount at different moments, in one window or several, then show the same pose and step together, so a `Steps` spinner shown many times draws one frame per step instead of one per step for each distinct start time. A run that mounts late starts part-way through an iteration, as a CSS animation with a negative delay does, and a finite iteration count ends on the clock. The delay counts from the run's start on the clock: a node that mounts within the delay of its period waits only for the rest of it. The start is computed once, when the run is created. Registering the same keyframes again with `register_keyframes` turns this off.

## 0.47.0 — 2026-10-16

Behaviour changes:

- Animation and transition frames are paced by when the animated value next changes. A pass that ticked a running animation used to leave `Tree::needs_redraw` set, and nothing cleared it, so the winit shell asked for the next frame at once and drew as fast as the swapchain let it, past its own refresh pacing: a turning 16 px loader laid out and drew the whole window 60 times a second (120 or 144 on faster displays), 10–16 % of a core in release in an app with a large tree. A pass now records when the running animations and transitions next draw something else (`Tree::next_timeline_change`), and the shell asks for the frame at that time (`request_redraw_at`), at most one frame per display refresh:
  - a span eased by `Easing::Steps { count, .. }` gets one frame per step: a 1 s turn in 12 steps presents 12 frames a second;
  - a delay draws nothing new until it ends;
  - a span that changes continuously (linear, cubic Bézier) still gets a frame every display refresh, now through the paced path: the loop sleeps until half a refresh after the last present, then the swapchain's acquire puts the frame on the next vblank, so every vblank shows a new frame as long as a frame's work stays under about half a refresh (8 ms at 60 Hz, 3.5 ms at 144 Hz). The price: input, MCP calls, timers and wakes from other threads that arrive while the pass waits in its acquire are handled after it, up to about half a refresh minus the frame's work. A span eased by `Easing::Steps` never waits in the acquire, so during it they are handled when they arrive; a click or a timer during any animation used to wait for the frame being presented, up to a whole refresh. With a present mode that does not wait for the display (`Mailbox`, `Immediate`), frames come at most every three quarters of a refresh;
  - a finished animation asks for no frame, and the loop waits as in 0.45.0 (`ControlFlow::WaitUntil` for the next timer or scheduled redraw, `ControlFlow::Wait` without one);
  - a fractional iteration count (`AnimationIterationCount::Count(1.5)`) gets its last frame when the run ends, and a span or transition eased by `StepPosition::JumpStart` or `JumpBoth` draws its first step as soon as it starts.
- A minimized window, or one that macOS reports occluded (`WindowEvent::Occluded`), draws no animation, transition or continuous image frames, its perf overlay takes no samples, and it drops the frames it had scheduled (an animation's next step, a caret's blink, an animated image's next frame). It used to draw them all at the display's rate. Its animations keep their clock: when the window shows again it draws a frame at once, with each animation where its time puts it, and paces the next ones as before. Its timers, futures, streams and input run as before. Windows reports a minimized window, but not one covered by other windows.
- `Tree::register_keyframes` requests a redraw, so a node that already plays the keyframes starts once they are registered.
- The paced frames of a window whose display reports no refresh rate come at 60 Hz; they came every 1 ms. The winit shell reads a display's refresh rate at most once a second, because the query reaches the display driver, and again when the window moves or its scale factor changes.

Breaking:

- After a pass that only animated, `Tree::needs_redraw()` is `false`. A shell that drives `Tree::pass` itself, and kept animations going by redrawing while `needs_redraw()` was true, asks for its next frame at `Tree::next_timeline_change()` instead, paced as the winit shell does: at `max(changes_at, presented + T/2, started + 3T/4)` for a display that refreshes every `T`, where `started` and `presented` are when the last frame's pass started and when its present returned. A pace of a whole refresh from the present adds each frame's work and wake latency to the period, so continuous animations drift past vblanks and judder.

Added:

- `Tree::next_timeline_change()`: when the running animations and transitions next draw something else, as of the last pass. A span that changes continuously returns the time of that pass; `None` when nothing runs.
- The `spinner_pacing` example (`--features winit,wgpu`): a page of text with one spinner that turns in 12 steps or smoothly, optionally minimized. It prints its pid and how many frames it presented, to measure what an animation costs.

## 0.46.0 — 2026-10-16

Behaviour changes:

- A left press on another dropdown's trigger while a popup or select menu is open now switches dropdowns in one press, like a menu bar. It closes the open one and is delivered to the trigger, which opens its dropdown and takes focus. It used to be consumed by `OutsidePress::Consume` (the default), so the person had to press the second trigger twice. The first interactive element under the pointer (focusable, focus-disabled, a button or a select) is a trigger when it is a `Select`, or the anchor itself of a `Popup`/`Popover`/`Overlay` with `dismiss_on_outside_click(true)` and a signal-backed open state, whether that popup is open or closed. Other elements inside an anchor, such as the other half of a split button, the items of a status bar, or a scrollbar over a trigger, are ordinary content. The press closes the consuming popups and the open selects before it is dispatched, so a trigger that prevents the default of its press still switches. A press on empty space or ordinary content still only closes the open dropdown. A press on the open popup's own trigger still reaches that trigger alone, so a toggle closes it; a select menu open inside that popup now closes with it, where the press used to close only the select menu. Add `Popup::switch_on_anchor_press(false)` (also on `Overlay`) to a popup whose anchor a left press does not open, such as a context menu or an autocomplete list; its anchor then is ordinary content. A popup that the app adds to the tree only while it is open has no anchor lurq can see while it is closed. Declare it always and drive it with `.open(signal)` so that its trigger switches in one press.

Breaking:

- A Markdown paragraph, heading or table cell with links in it is one rich-text node, links included, so it wraps and selects as one text. A link is no longer a text node with its own box: it is a child node of its paragraph with no text style of its own, placed on its words, whose bounds (`ElementRef`, `find_element(...).bounds()`, MCP refs) are its first laid-out line.
